. By Proposition
3.11
, the extremal rays of
\operatorname{Mov}_{1}(\mathcal{TL}_{3})
are generated by

(3.40)

\displaystyle(1,0,0,0,0),\ (2,0,-1,0,0),\ (2,0,0,0,-1),\ (2,0,-1,0,-1),\ (3,-1,0,0,0),

\displaystyle(3,0,0,-1,0),\ (3,-1,0,-1,0),\ (6,0,-3,-2,0),\ (6,-2,0,0,-3).

Remark 3.41

(Maple implementation)
.

The Maple worksheets used for the polyhedral computations on
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
are available at

https://github.com/msslxa/Kausz

They implement the grading matrix of the Cox ring of
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
and compute the cones

\operatorname{Eff}(\mathcal{TL}_{n})
,
\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{TL}_{n})
, and
\operatorname{Nef}(\mathcal{TL}_{n})
, together with the rays of the cones of effective and moving curves.

4.
Stable Maps in Lagrangian Grassmannians

Based on
[
41
,
32
,
40
]
, we recall certain basic notation and properties of the moduli spaces of stable maps to Lagrangian Grassmannians.

Let
\beta\in H_{2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\mathbb{Z})
be an effective curve class. Let

M_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\beta)
be the set of isomorphism classes of pointed maps
(C,p_{1},\cdots,p_{k},f)
, where
C
is a nonsingular rational curve, the markings
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}
are
distinct points of
C
, and
f
is a morphism from
C
to
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
satisfying
f_{*}([C])=\beta
.
Let
\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\beta)
denote the Kontsevich moduli space of
k
–pointed, genus
0
stable maps to
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
of class
\beta
.

Since
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
is convex, namely,
H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1},f^{*}T_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)})=0
for every morphism
f\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
,
\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\beta)
is normal, irreducible and has the expected dimension:

(4.1)

\dim\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\beta)\;=\;\dim\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)+c_{1}(T_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)})\cdot\beta+k-3.

We will concern the case where
\beta=\beta_{n}
is the homology class of the closure of a general
\mathbb{C}^{*}
–orbit. Equivalently,
\beta_{n}
is the unique effective class such that
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1))\cdot\beta_{n}=n
.
For notational simplicity, following the usual convention in Gromov–Witten theory, we write
\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n):=\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\beta_{n})
.

For each
k\geq 1
, there are evaluation morphisms

\operatorname{ev}_{i}\colon\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\longrightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\qquad 1\leq i\leq k,

defined by
\operatorname{ev}_{i}\bigl((C,p_{1},\dots,p_{k},f)\bigr):=f(p_{i})
. We then have for
k=2
the evaluation morphism

\operatorname{ev}=(\operatorname{ev}_{1},\operatorname{ev}_{2})\colon\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\longrightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)\times\operatorname{LG}(n,2n).

Definition 4.2
.

For
p,q\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
we denote by
F_{p,q}:=\operatorname{ev}^{-1}(p,q)\subset\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
the fiber of the two–point evaluation map over
(p,q)
with the reduced scheme structure.

In the following, we show that for a general pair
(p,q)
the fiber
F_{p,q}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
.

Before proceeding, we recall modular properties of the Kontsevich spaces more precisely. A
k
–pointed map
(C,p_{1},\cdots,p_{k},f)
is a morphism
f\colon C\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
, where
C
denotes a tree of projective lines with
k
distinct marked points that are smooth points of
C
.
(C,p_{1},\cdots,p_{k},f)
is isomorphic to
(C^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,p_{k}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if there is a scheme isomorphism
\tau\colon C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
taking
p_{i}
to
p^{\prime}_{i}
with
f^{\prime}\circ\tau=f
.
A
k
-pointed map
f\colon C\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
is called Kontsevich stable if any irreducible component mapped to a point is stable as a pointed curve.

A family of
k
-pointed maps over a base scheme
B
is a diagram

(4.3)

\begin{CD}\mathfrak{X}@>{f}>{}>\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)\\
@V{\pi}V{}V\\
B\end{CD}

together with
k
disjoint sections
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\colon B\to\mathfrak{X}
such that:

-

\pi
is a flat family of trees of smooth rational curves;

-

the sections
\sigma_{i}
do not meet the singularities of the fibers of
\pi
.

Thus for each
b\in B
, the restriction
f_{b}\colon\mathfrak{X}_{b}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
is a
k
-pointed map, with marked points
\sigma_{1}(b),\dots,\sigma_{k}(b)
. Two families of maps over
B
,

(\pi\colon\mathfrak{X}\to B,\{\sigma_{i}\},f)
,
(\pi^{\prime}:\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}\to B,\{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\},f^{\prime})
,
are isomorphic if there exists a scheme isomorphism
\tau:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
satisfying:
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\tau
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}=\tau\circ\sigma_{i}
,
f=f^{\prime}\circ\tau
.

Let
\overline{M}_{0,n}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\subset\overline{M}_{0,n}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
denote the open locus of stable maps with no nontrivial automorphisms. By
[
32
, Theorem 2]
,

\overline{M}_{0,n}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is a nonsingular, fine moduli space (for automorphism–free stable maps) equipped with a universal family

(4.4)

\Pi\colon\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,n}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n).

This means that for any family
\mathcal{X}\to B
of such curves, there exists a unique morphism
\kappa\colon B\to M
such that the pullback
\kappa^{*}\mathcal{U}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{X}
as families over
B
.

Applying Lemma
2.23
, Proposition
2.24
and marking
p_{1}=p_{+}
,
p_{2}=p_{-}
, we derive from the morphism
\mathrm{P}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TL}_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
a family of
2
–pointed stable maps with fibers embedded into
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
and with the morphism
f
in (
4.3
) given by
\mathrm{R}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TL}_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
. Then there is a unique morphism

(4.5)

i\colon\mathsf{CQ}_{n}\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\subset\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)

that pulls back the family. The morphism is bijective onto its image by Property (d) in Lemma
2.23
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
q\in\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
be general and let
Z_{q}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the corresponding fiber of
\mathrm{P}_{n}
.
Then the restriction of the tautological subbundle
\mathcal{S}
on
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
satisfies

\mathcal{S}|_{\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})}\ \cong\ \mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus n}.

Consequently,

T_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}|_{\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})}\ \cong\ \mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)^{\oplus\dim\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})/\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}\ \cong\ \mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)^{\oplus(\dim\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)-1)}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.23
(c) the curve
\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})
is a smooth degree
n
embedding
\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
passing through
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.
By Lemma
2.25
, after a change of parameter on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
we may write it in the big cell around
p_{-}
as the row span of a matrix of the form (
2.26
) with

f_{ij}(t)=a_{ij}\,t
for a symmetric matrix
A=(a_{ij})
.
Since the Plücker coordinate
P_{I^{*}}
vanishes to order
n
at
t=0
, we have
\det(A)\neq 0
, hence
A
is invertible and the point
t=\infty
maps to
p_{+}
.

Writing homogeneous coordinates
[u:v]
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(so
t=v/u
), the family of Lagrangian subspaces along the curve is the image of the rank
n
subbundle

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus n}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus 2n},\qquad(u,v)\ \longmapsto\ \bigl(u\,I_{n}\ \ \ v\,A\bigr),

whose fiber over
[u:v]
is exactly the corresponding
n
–plane in
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
.
Therefore
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus n}
.

Finally, for the Lagrangian Grassmannian one has
T_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}\simeq\Sym^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\vee})
, hence

T_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}|_{\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)^{\oplus\dim\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}
,
and the normal bundle is obtained by quotienting out
T_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)
.
∎

Theorem 4.7
.

For generic
p,q\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
,
F_{p,q}\subset\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
. Moreover, (
4.5
) induces an isomorphism from
\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
to
F_{p,q}
.

Proof.

There exists an automorphism
g
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
such that
g\cdot p_{+}=p,g\cdot p_{-}=q
, where
p_{+},p_{-}
are defined by (
2.7
). Then the induced action on
\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
restricts to an isomorphism
between
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
and
F_{p,q}
.
Hence, in what follows, we concentrate on
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
.

It is clear that
i(\mathsf{CQ}_{n})\subset F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
. We proceed to prove that

F_{p_{+},p_{-}}\subset i(\mathsf{CQ}_{n})
.

Take a generic geometric fiber
Z_{q}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
of
\mathrm{P}_{n}
, and set
C_{q}:=\mathrm{R}_{n}(Z_{q})\subset\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
.
By
[
56
]
,
{\rm Hilb}_{nt+1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))
is irreducible and smooth.
By Lemma
4.6
we have

N_{C_{q}/\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}\ \cong\ \mathcal{O}(2)^{\oplus\left(\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-1\right)}.

Hence the tangent space to the Hilbert scheme at
[C_{q}]
, namely
H^{0}(C_{q},N_{C_{q}/\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)})
, has dimension
\frac{3n(n+1)}{2}-3
, which equals
\dim{\rm Hilb}_{nt+1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))
.

Let
M^{0}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\subset\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
denote the locus of degree
n
embeddings
\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
.
Noticing that
\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is irreducible and
\dim\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)=3\dim\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)+2-3
by (
4.1
), we conclude that
M^{0}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is an open dense subset of
\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.

Suppose that there is a point
x\in F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
with
x\notin i(\mathsf{CQ}_{n})
. Then there exists a sequence of points
y_{1},y_{2},\cdots\in M^{0}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
such that
\lim_{i=1}^{\infty}y_{i}=x
. Following the same argument as in Lemma
2.29
, we can conclude that for
i
large enough, there exists an automorphism
\tau
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
, which is very close to the identity, such that
\tau(\mathfrak{X}_{y_{i}})
passing through
p_{+},p_{-}
and
\tau(y_{i})\notin i(\mathsf{CQ}_{n})
. However, by Lemma
2.25
, we must have
\tau(y_{i})\in i(\mathsf{CQ}_{n})
, which is a contradiction.

The action defined by (
2.1
) induces a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
–action on
\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.
Set-theoretically, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
–fixed locus in
\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is precisely
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
.

Since
\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is smooth and
\mathbb{C}^{*}
is linearly reductive, its fixed locus is smooth: at a fixed point
x
, the tangent space of the fixed locus is the invariant subspace
T_{x}(\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*})^{\mathbb{C}^{*}}
.
Hence
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
is smooth.

Finally, the morphism
i\colon\mathsf{CQ}_{n}\to F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
is bijective by construction, and it is proper because
\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
is projective and
\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is separated.
Therefore
i
is quasi-finite and proper, hence finite.
Since
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}
is smooth (in particular normal), Zariski’s Main Theorem implies that
i
is an isomorphism.

∎

Forgetting the
k
–th marked point and stabilizing the domain curve when necessary, we obtain forgetful maps

\phi_{k}\colon\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),d)\longrightarrow\overline{M}_{0,k-1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),d).

Michałek–Monin–Wiśniewski
[
48
, Corollary 3.11]
realized
\mathsf{CQ}_{n}

as the connected component of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
–fixed locus of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
. We recover such construction by forgetting the marked points as follows.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
i
be given by (
4.5
). The composition

(4.9)

\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}\circ i\colon\mathsf{CQ}_{n}\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)

is a closed embedding. In other words,
\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
is realized as a closed subvariety of the unpointed Kontsevich moduli space by forgetting the two marked points of the stable maps passing through
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
. Consequently, for any generic points
p,q\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
, we have the closed embedding

(\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2})|_{F_{p,q}}\colon F_{p,q}\longrightarrow\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.7
,
F_{p,q}\subset\overline{M}_{0,2}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
. Since set-theoriatically
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}
is given by forgetting the marked points and stabilizing the domain curve, we conclude that
(\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2})(F_{p,q})\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.
Let
\overline{M}_{0,0}^{emb}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
denote the open locus of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
parametrizing stable maps
f\colon C\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
where
f
is a closed embedding. Then by sending a stable map to its image, we have a natural forgetful morphism

(4.10)

\Phi:\overline{M}_{0,0}^{emb}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hilb}_{nt+1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)).

By Proposition
2.24
,

\Phi\circ\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}\circ i\colon\mathsf{CQ}_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hilb}_{nt+1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))

is a closed embedding. Then (
4.9
) is a closed embedding.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
U\subset\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)\times\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
be the open locus of pairs
(p,q)
for which
Theorems
4.7
and
4.8
hold. Varying
(p,q)\in U
the images
(\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2})(F_{p,q})
sweep an open dense locus in
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.

Proof.

Since the morphism (
4.10
) induces an open embedding on
\overline{M}_{0,0}^{*}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
, Corollary
4.11
follows directly from Claim
2.30
.
∎

Set
M_{p,q}:=(\phi_{3})^{-1}(F_{p,q})\subset\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
, which parametrizes
3
–pointed stable maps
(C,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},f)
such that
f(p_{1})=p
and
f(p_{2})=q
. Here
\phi_{3}\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)

is the morphism forgetting the last marked point.

Theorem 4.12
.

For a general pair
(p,q)
there is an isomorphism
M_{p,q}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \mathcal{TL}_{n}
over
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
. Under this identification, the restricted evaluation morphism
\operatorname{ev}_{3}|_{M_{p,q}}\colon M_{p,q}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
equals the blow–up

\mathrm{R}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TL}_{n}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
p=p_{+}
and
q=p_{-}
. Since
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}\subset\overline{M}^{*}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
, then by
[
44
, Theorem 4.5 of Chapter V]
,
M_{p_{+},p_{-}}
is isomorphic to the universal family over
F_{p_{+},p_{-}}\cong\mathsf{CQ}_{n}
, which is
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
. By consider the stabilization process pointwise, it is clear that
ev_{3}|_{M_{p_{+},p_{-}}}
coincides with the morphism from the universal family
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
to its image in
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
, namely the blow–up
\mathrm{R}_{n}
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

For each
x\in\mathcal{TL}_{n}
, the
corresponding stable map in
\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
is represented by
[\alpha\colon C\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]
, where
C
is the fiber of
\mathrm{P}_{n}
over
\mathrm{P}_{n}(x)
,
\alpha
is the restriction of
\mathrm{R}_{n}
to
C
, and the first marking is the unique intersection point
(\mathrm{P}_{n})^{-1}(\mathrm{P}_{n}(x))\cap D^{+}_{0}
, the second one is
(\mathrm{P}_{n})^{-1}(\mathrm{P}_{n}(x))\cap D^{-}_{0}
, and the third one is
x
. When
x
is a node or coincides with the first two markings, one needs to stabilize the domain curve.

By Theorem
4.12
, we may identify
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
with
M_{p,q}\subset\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
. Denote by
\phi\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
the morphism forgetting the first two markings. Then, restricting
\phi
to
M_{p,q}
yields a morphism

\phi|_{M_{p,q}}\colon\mathcal{TL}_{n}\cong M_{p,q}\longrightarrow\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.

Corollary 4.14
.

For a general pair
(p,q)
,
\phi|_{M_{p,q}}

is a closed embedding.

Proof.

We may assume that
p=p_{+}
and
q=p_{-}
.
Then, we have the following commutative diagram:

(4.15)

Since
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}\circ i
is a closed embedding by Theorem
4.8
, then Corollary
4.14
follows.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

As the pair
(p,q)
varies, the images
\phi(M_{p,q})
, each isomorphic to
\mathcal{TL}_{n}
, cover a open dense subset of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),n)
.

Proof.

By (
4.15
),
\phi(M_{p,q})=(\phi_{1})^{-1}(F_{p,q})
. We then complete the proof by Corollary
4.11
.
∎

5.
Birational Geometry of Moduli Spaces of Pointed Conics in
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)

We begin by recalling several divisor classes on the Kontsevich moduli space
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
.

For the Lagrangian Grassmannian
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)=\operatorname{LG}(V,\omega)
, fix a complete isotropic flag of subspaces

\{0\}=F^{0}\subset F^{1}\subset F^{2}\subset\dots\subset F^{n}\subset V
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{n}
be the set of strict partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{l})
with
0<\lambda_{l}<\dots<\lambda_{1}\leq n
and denote by
\left|\lambda\right|=\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{l}
the weight of
\lambda
. For each
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
there is a codimension–
\left|\lambda\right|
Schubert variety
\Sigma^{n}_{\lambda}\subset\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
defined by

\Sigma^{n}_{\lambda}:=\{W\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),\,\dim(W\cap F^{n+1-\lambda_{i}})\geq i,\;i=1,\dots,l\}.

Denote by
\sigma_{2}\in A^{2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))
the unique codimension–
2
Schubert class in the Chow group with rational coefficients. Define the tautological class

H_{\sigma_{2}}\ :=\ (\phi_{2})_{*}(\operatorname{ev}_{2}^{*}\sigma_{2})\ \in\ A^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\cong\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\otimes\mathbb{Q},

where
\phi_{2}\colon\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\to\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is the morphism forgetting the last marking, and
\operatorname{ev}_{2}:\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
is the evaluation morphism at the second marking.

Define the evaluation class

H_{1}:=\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1))\in\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\otimes\mathbb{Q},

where
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
is the evaluation morphism and
\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)
is the Plücker line bundle on
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
.

The boundary of
\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),d)
is formed by maps whose domains are nodal (reducible) genus
0
curves, which is a divisor with normal crossings (up to a finite group quotient) by
[
32
]
. Specifically,
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
contains a unique boundary divisor,
\Delta_{1|1}
. This divisor is the closure of the locus of stable maps
[\alpha\colon(C,x)\to\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)]
such that

C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
has two irreducible components meeting in one node and
\deg(\alpha|_{C_{1}})=\deg(\alpha|_{C_{2}})=1
. Analogously, the unmarked moduli space
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
possesses a unique boundary divisor, which we denote by
\Delta^{n}
.

In what follows, we shall define the unbalanced divisor
D_{\mathrm{unb}}
on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
as a natural effective
\mathbb{Q}
–Cartier divisor.
Note that since
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
possesses only quotient singularities, it suffices to define
D_{\unb}
as a Weil divisor.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)=\operatorname{LG}(V,\omega)
be a morphism of Plücker degree
2
. Then there exists an isotropic subspace

H_{\alpha}\subset V
of dimension
n-2
such that
H_{\alpha}\subset\alpha(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Equivalently, the image of
\alpha

is contained in the closed subvariety

\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{H_{\alpha}}:=\{\,L\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)\mid H_{\alpha}\subset L\,\}\ \subset\ \operatorname{LG}(n,2n),

and there is a canonical identification
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{H_{\alpha}}\cong\operatorname{LG}(H_{\alpha}^{\perp}/H_{\alpha})\cong\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{S}
the tautological bundle of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
. The pullback of the tautological bundle
E_{\alpha}:=\alpha^{*}\mathcal{S}
is a rank
n
subbundle of the trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. Since the degree of
\alpha
is
2
,
E_{\alpha}
splits as
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus(n-2)}
or
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-2)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus(n-1)}
. In particular,
E_{\alpha}
contains a trivial subbundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus(n-2)}
. Then the linear independent constant sections of
E_{\alpha}
yield the desired
(n-2)
–dimensional isotropic subspace
H_{\alpha}\subset V
.

Now for each
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\alpha(t)
corresponds to an isotropic
n
–plane
\Lambda_{t}
satisfying
H_{\alpha}\subset\Lambda\subset H_{\alpha}^{\perp}
.
By the symplectic reduction, the space of such isotropic
n
–planes is isomorphic to the Lagrangian Grassmannian of the
4
–dimensional symplectic quotient
H^{\perp}/H
:

(5.2)

\{\Lambda\in\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)\mid H\subset\Lambda\}\cong\operatorname{LG}(H^{\perp}/H)\cong\operatorname{LG}(2,4).

We derive the isomorphism
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{H_{\alpha}}\cong\operatorname{LG}(H_{\alpha}^{\perp}/H_{\alpha})\cong\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.
∎

When
n=2
, we define
D_{\unb}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
as the closure of the locus of the stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(2,4)]
such that
E_{\alpha}\not\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus 2}
.

When
n\geq 3
, by associating
\alpha
to the
(n-2)
–plane

H_{\alpha}\subset V\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
, we derive a rational map to the isotropic Grassmannian of
(n-2)
–planes in
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
:

(5.3)

\begin{split}\xi\ \ \colon\ \ \overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)&\dashrightarrow\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)\\
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]&\mapsto H_{\alpha}\end{split}\ \ \ .

Recall that the indeterminacy locus of (
5.3
) in
M_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
consists precisely of stable maps with splitting type
E_{\alpha}\cong\mathcal{O}(-2)\oplus\mathcal{O}^{\oplus(n-1)}
. Such a map
[\alpha]
is necessarily a double cover
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\ell\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
of a line
\ell\subset\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)
.
By (
4.1
), the dimension of the space of lines in
\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)
is
\frac{1}{2}n(n+1)+n-2
. Accounting for the two degrees of freedom in choosing branch points for a double cover
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we find the codimension of this locus in
M_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
to be
n-1
, which is at least
2
for
n\geq 3
.

Moreover, the rational map (
5.3
) is well–defined at a generic point of the boundary divisor
\Delta^{n}
in the following manner.
Suppose
[\alpha]
is a stable map whose domain is a union of two irreducible components
C_{1},C_{2}
meeting at a node, and whose image is a pair of distinct intersecting lines
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)
.
The intersection of all isotropic subspaces contained in
L_{1}\cup L_{2}
defines a uniqe
(n-2)
–dimensional isotropic subspace.

Now we define the unbalanced divisor
D_{\mathrm{unb}}
as the pullback of the Schubert divisor class via
\xi
, namely,

D_{\mathrm{unb}}:=\xi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)}(1)
.
More explicitly, fix a general
(n+2)
-dimensional linear subspace
V_{n+2}\subset V\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
and let
\Sigma\subset\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
be the Schubert divisor defined by the incidence condition
\dim(H\cap V_{n+2})\geq 1
.
Away from a closed subset of codimension at least
2
, the rational map
\xi
is a morphism; thus, the preimage
\xi^{-1}(\Sigma)
on the regular locus extends uniquely to a Weil divisor on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
. Notice that the definition of
D_{\mathrm{unb}}
is independent of the choice of
V_{n+2}
.

It is clear that the restriction of (
5.3
) to the boundary divisor
\Delta^{n}
is dominant onto
\mathrm{SG}(n-2,2n)
, and hence
D_{\mathrm{unb}}\cap\Delta^{n}
is of codimension at least
2
. Therefore,
D_{\mathrm{unb}}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is the closure of the locus of stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]
such that
\dim(H_{\alpha}\cap V_{n+2})\geq 1
.

Definition 5.4
.

For
n\geq 2
, we define the unbalanced divisor on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
as the pullback
\phi_{1}^{*}(D_{\unb})
of the unbalanced locus in
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
, where
\phi_{1}
is the forgetful morphism. With a slight abuse of notation, we also denote this pullback as
D_{\unb}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

For
n\geq 2
and
k=0,1
, the locus
D_{\unb}\subset\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is an irreducible divisor.

Proof.

For
k=0
, we provide a detailed proof based on
[
17
, Section 6]
as follows.

When
n=2
, the divisor
D_{\unb}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
coincides with the
closure of the locus of degree
2
stable maps that are double covers of a line

\ell\subset\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
. Equivalently, such a map factors as a double cover

\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\ell\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
branched along
2
points, followed by the inclusion
\ell\hookrightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
. The Fano variety of lines in
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
.
For each line
\ell
,
the space of double covers is parameterized by unordered pairs of distinct branch points on
\ell
, which is the irreducible variety

((\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\backslash\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Thus,
D_{\unb}
contains an open dense subset that is a
((\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\backslash\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
–bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. In particular,
D_{\unb}
is irreducible.

Assume
n\geq 3
. To establish the irreducibility of
D_{\unb}
, it suffices to show that the locus
D_{\unb}^{0}
, consisting of stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)]
with splitting type
E_{\alpha}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}^{\oplus(n-2)}
, is irreducible. First notice that the Schubert divisor
\Sigma\subset\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
, defined by the fixed generic vector space
V_{n+2}
, is irreducible.

Fix an arbitrary point
H\in\Sigma
. Then,
\xi
is well–defined on
D_{\unb}^{0}
, and the fiber
\xi^{-1}(H)\cap D_{\unb}^{0}
consists of all degree
2
stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)]
such that
\alpha(t)
corresponds to an isotropic
n
–plane
\Lambda_{t}
satisfying
H\subset\Lambda\subset H^{\perp}
, for any
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
By Lemma
5.1
, the fiber
\xi^{-1}(H)\cap D_{\unb}^{0}
is isomorphic to an open subset of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
. We thus conclude that
D_{\unb}
is irreducible.

Now consider the pullback
\phi_{1}^{*}(D_{\unb})
. Notice that the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1|1}
is mapped onto the boundary divisor
\Delta^{n}
by the forgetful morphism
\phi_{1}\colon\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
.
Since
\Delta^{n}
is not contained in
D_{\unb}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
is not contained in
\phi_{1}^{*}(D_{\unb})
. On the other hand, over the open locus
{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
, the fiber of the forgetful morphism

\phi_{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
: forgetting the marked point from a smooth domain curve does not require stabilization in this situation. Then,
\phi_{1}^{*}(D_{\unb})
is also irreducible.
∎

Recall the construction in Corollary
4.16
. Consider the evaluation morphism

\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}:=(\operatorname{ev}_{1},\operatorname{ev}_{2})\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)\to\operatorname{LG}(2,4)\times\operatorname{LG}(2,4).

Set
U:=(\operatorname{LG}(2,4)\times\operatorname{LG}(2,4))\setminus\Sigma_{1}
, where
\Sigma_{1}\subset\operatorname{LG}(2,4)\times\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
is the locus of collinear pairs
(p,q)
, namely, pairs lying on a line

\ell\subset\operatorname{LG}(2,4)\cong Q^{3}
.
For
(p,q)\in U
, the fiber
M_{p,q}=f^{-1}(p,q)
is isomorphic to the blow–up
\mathcal{TL}_{2}
by Theorem
4.12
and Property (b) in Lemma
2.27
. For convenience, via Example
3.37
we write
\mathrm{R}_{2}\colon\mathcal{TL}_{2}\to\operatorname{LG}(2,4)

as
\mathrm{R}_{2}\colon\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})\rightarrow Q^{3}
, and the exceptional divisors as
E_{p},E_{q}
. Write
H:=(\mathrm{R}_{2})^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(2,4)}(1)
.

By Corollary
4.14
, the restriction of the morphism
\phi\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
forgetting the first two markings
yields a closed embedding

\iota_{p,q}:\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})\cong M_{p,q}\ \hookrightarrow\ \overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2).

Furthermore, the family

\left\{\iota_{p,q}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))\right\}_{(p,q)\in U}

covers an open dense subset of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(2,4),2)
by Corollary
4.16
. It is clear that this subset consists of stable maps whose image is not a line.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
(p,q)\in U
, we have the following equalities in
\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
:

(5.7)

\iota_{p,q}^{*}(H_{1})=H,\ \iota_{p,q}^{*}(\Delta_{1|1})=2\bigl(H-E_{p}-E_{q}\bigr),\ \iota_{p,q}^{*}(D_{\unb})=0.

Proof.

According to (
4.15
), we have
\phi^{*}(H_{1})=\operatorname{ev}_{3}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(2,4)}(1)
on
\overline{M}_{0,3}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
, where
\operatorname{ev}_{3}
is the evaluation at the third marking. By Theorem
4.12
, on the fiber
M_{p,q}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})
, the map
\operatorname{ev}_{3}
coincides with the blow–up
\mathrm{R}_{2}
. Therefore,
\iota_{p,q}^{*}(H_{1})=\operatorname{ev}_{3}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(2,4)}(1)|_{M_{p,q}}=(\mathrm{R}_{2})^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(2,4)}(1)=H
.

By Remark
4.13
, for each point
x\in\mathcal{TL}_{2}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})
, the
corresponding stable map of
\iota_{p,q}(x)\in\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
is represented by
[\alpha\colon C\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)]
, where
C=(\mathrm{P}_{2})^{-1}(\mathrm{P}_{2}(x))
,
\alpha=\mathrm{R}_{2}|_{C}
, and the marking is
x
; when
x
is a node, one needs to stabilize the domain curve. Then, we conclude that
\iota_{p,q}^{-1}(\Delta_{1|1})
is supported on the union of the boundary divisors
D^{+}_{1}
and
D^{-}_{1}
(see Definition
2.12
). Due to the symmetry between
p
and
q
, the pullback of the divisor class
\Delta_{1|1}
takes the form
\iota_{p,q}^{*}(\Delta_{1|1})=m(D^{+}_{1}+D^{-}_{1})
for some positive integer
m
.

We claim that
m=1
. It suffices to check that at a generic point of
\iota_{p,q}(D^{+}_{1})
, the image
\iota_{p,q}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))
intersects the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1,1}
transversely. Take a generic point
x\in D^{+}_{1}
. Note that
\Delta_{1|1}
is smooth at
\iota_{p,q}(x)
, for the corresponding stable map has no nontrivial automorphisms. It then suffices to produce a smooth analytic curve in
\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})
through
x
whose image under
\iota_{p,q}
intersects
\Delta_{1,1}
transversely. Take a smooth analytic curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{TL}_{2}
from a unit disc such that
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma
intersects with
D^{+}_{1}
transversely at
x
. By shrinking
\mathbb{D}
, we may further assume that the restriction of the projection
\mathrm{P}_{2}
on
\gamma
is an embedding.
Noticing that the universal family
(\mathrm{P}_{2})^{-1}(\mathrm{P}_{2}(\gamma(\mathbb{D})))
is smooth, we conclude that
\iota_{p,q}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))
intersects the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1,1}
transversely at
x
.

Therefore, by (
3.3
), we have that
\iota_{p,q}^{*}(\Delta_{1|1})=D^{+}_{1}+D^{-}_{1}=(H-2E_{p})+(H-2E_{q})=2(H-E_{p}-E_{q})
.

Furthermore, since for
(p,q)\in U
the image conic of any map in
M_{p,q}
is not a line,
M_{p,q}
is disjoint from the unbalanced locus. It follows that
\iota_{p,q}^{*}(D_{\unb})=0
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

\{\Delta_{1|1},H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}}\}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
–basis of

\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

By
[
52
, Proposition 1]
, every class in
\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is generated by the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1|1}
, the evaluation class
H_{1}
, and the tautological class
H_{\sigma_{2}}
.

By a slight abuse of notation, we denote by
\Delta_{1|1}
,
H_{1}
, and
D_{\unb}
their images in the Néron–Severi space
N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(2,4),2))_{\mathbb{R}}
, respectively. It suffices to show that they are
\mathbb{Q}
–linearly independent.

Suppose there is a linear relation

aH_{1}+b\Delta_{1|1}+cD_{\unb}=0

in
N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(2,4),2))_{\mathbb{R}}
. By Lemma
5.6
, for a general pair
(p,q)\in U
, pulling back by
\iota_{p,q}
yields

aH+2b(H-E_{p}-E_{q})=0
.
Since the classes
H
,
E_{p}
,
E_{q}
are linearly independent in
\mathrm{Pic}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, we must have
a=b=0
. On the other hand, since
D_{\unb}
is effective and nonempty, we have
c=0
. The proof is complete.
∎

Theorem 5.9
.

The cone of effective divisor classes of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
is

\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))=\langle H_{1},\Delta_{1|1},D_{\unb}\rangle\ \subset\ N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))_{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

Since
H_{1}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
, and
D_{\unb}
are effective,
\langle H_{1},\Delta_{1|1},D_{\unb}\rangle\subset\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))
.
To prove the reverse inclusion, we take an arbitrary prime effective divisor

D\subset\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
that is different from
D_{\unb}
and
\Delta_{1|1}
. According to the proof of Proposition
5.11
, we express
D
in the Néron–Severi space as the linear combination

D\equiv\alpha H_{1}+\beta\Delta_{1|1}+\gamma D_{\mathrm{unb}}
.

Fix a smooth conic
C
in
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
. Define a curve
\Gamma\subset\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
as the locus of stable maps
[f\colon(\mathbb{P}^{1},x)\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)]
such that
f
is an isomorphism from
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
C
. Computing the dimensions of the Kontsevich spaces and the Hilbert schemes as in the proof of Theorem
4.7
, we can conclude by Claim
2.30
and (
4.10
) that
the locus of stable maps
[f\colon(\mathbb{P}^{1},x)\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)]
such that
f
is an embedding and
f(\mathbb{P}^{1})
passes through a pair of generic points is open in
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(2,4),2)
. Then, it is clear that
\Gamma
is a moving curve with
\Gamma\cdot H=2
and
\Gamma\cdot\Delta_{1|1}=\Gamma\cdot D_{\unb}=0
. Therefore
\alpha\geq 0
.

Choose a general pair
(p,q)
so that
\iota_{p,q}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))\not\subset D
. Then the pullback
\iota_{p,q}^{*}(D)
is an effective divisor on
\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})
.
We claim that
\beta\geq 0
. Otherwise, we take the effective curve
\zeta^{+}_{1}
as in Definition
3.7
. By Lemma
3.8
, we get that

\zeta^{+}_{1}\cdot H=0
,
\zeta^{+}_{1}\cdot(H-E_{p}-E_{q})=1
, and hence

\zeta^{+}_{1}\cdot\iota_{p,q}^{*}(D)=\beta<0
. Then
\iota_{p,q}(\zeta^{+}_{1})\subset D
.
Take a generic point
[f\colon(\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1},x)\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)]
of
\Delta_{1|1}
which is not in
D
, and a pair of points
(f(x),q)
that can be transformed to
(p_{+},p_{-})
by an automorphism of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
. Then
\iota_{x,q}([\alpha\colon(\mathbb{P}^{1},x)\rightarrow\operatorname{LG}(2,4)])\in D
, which is a contradiction.

Finally, we shall prove that
\gamma\geq 0
by the following morphism from the Hirzebruch surface
F_{1}
to the quadric threefold
Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
defined by
z_{0}z_{4}-z_{1}z_{3}+z_{2}^{2}=0
.

Let
\Sigma\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}
be the fan of
X_{\Sigma}\cong F_{1}
, with primitive generators of rays
\mathbf{v}_{a}=(1,1)
,
\mathbf{v}_{v}=(0,1)
,
\mathbf{v}_{b}=(-1,0)
and
\mathbf{v}_{u}=(0,-1)
. The lattice
N\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is a sublattice of
N^{\prime}=N+(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z},0)
of index
2
. Deonte
\Sigma^{\prime}
the same fan but in
N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then the toric variety
X_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
associated to
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a weighted blow up of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.
(N,\Sigma)\to(N^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is a double cover in the sense of
[
1
, Definition 3.2]
, so on geometry aspect we obtain the double cover
f\colon X_{\Sigma}\to X_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
. For
\Sigma^{\prime}
, remove the ray generated by
\mathbf{v}_{v}
, which corresponds to the contraction of the
(-\frac{1}{2})
-section on
X_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, denoted by
g\colon X_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
. Since
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)=\mathbf{V}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}^{2})\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}_{[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}:z_{3}]}
,
g\circ f
maps
F_{1}
to
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)\subset Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
. Here we view
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
as a generic conic ruling over a line instead of a ruling over a conic curve.

Equivalently, by Cox quotient construction of toric varieties, the map
F_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
can also be interpreted via the lift
\mathbb{A}^{4}_{a,b,u,v}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
. Explicitly, the quotient map is
(\mathbb{A}^{4}_{a,b,u,v}\setminus\mathbf{V}(B(\Sigma)))/G\to F_{1}
, where
G\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
and the unstable locus
\mathbf{V}(B(\Sigma))=\mathbb{A}_{a,b}^{2}\cup\mathbb{A}^{2}_{u,v}
. The weights of the
G
-action are
\mathrm{wt}(a)=\mathrm{wt}(b)=(1,0)
,
\mathrm{wt}(u)=(0,1)
and
\mathrm{wt}(v)=(-1,1)
. So the lifted morphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{4}_{a,b,u,v}\setminus\mathbf{V}(B(\Sigma))

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{P}^{3}_{z},

\displaystyle(a,b,u,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto[u^{2}:0:buv:a^{2}v^{2}:b^{2}v^{2}]

descends to
F_{1}\to Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
. It is a double cover and the contraction is
(\mathbb{A}^{3}_{a,b,u}\setminus\mathbf{V}(B(\Sigma)))/G\mapsto[u^{2}:0:0:0:0]
. Therefore, we can construct a family of stable map
C\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
with the contracted curve as the marking. Since the generic fiber is a smooth conic and a special fiber is a double cover of a line,
\gamma\geq 0
.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

In general, determining an extremal ray of the effective cone requires a suitable moving curve, as in
[
20
, Lemma 2.4]
. However, such a moving curve may not admit a constant marking. In the proof above, we therefore use a non–moving test curve to establish that
\beta\geq 0
, by exploiting the geometry of
\mathcal{TL}_{2}
.

The following results aim to establish a link between the divisor theory of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
and that of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
for
n\geq 3
.
Fix an isotropic
(n-2)
–plane
A_{0}\subset V
. The inclusion
\imath_{A_{0}}\colon\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
yields a morphism

\jmath_{A_{0},k}\colon\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)\ \cong\ \overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}},2)\ \longrightarrow\ \overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)

for each
k\geq 0
,
whose image is the locus of stable maps whose image is contained in
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}}
.
Then,

Proposition 5.11
.

The pullback homomorphism

\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}\colon\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ \longrightarrow\ \operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is an isomorphism which sends the divisor classes
H_{1}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
,
H_{\sigma_{2}}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
to
H_{1}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
,
H_{\sigma_{2}}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
, respectively. Consequently,
\{\Delta_{1|1},H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}}\}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
–basis of
\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. The induced map on numerical divisor classes

\overline{\jmath}^{*}_{A_{0},1}:N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))_{\mathbb{R}}

is an isomorphism that sends the divisor class
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
to the class
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the argument for unmarked moduli spaces given in
[
17
, Proposition 6.4]
.

By
[
52
, Proposition 1]
,
\operatorname{Pic}\bigl(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\bigr)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is generated by
[\Delta_{1|1}]
,
H_{1}
, and
H_{\sigma_{2}}
.

Since
\imath_{A_{0}}
pulls back the unique codimension–
2
Schubert class of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
to the unique codimension–
2
Schubert class of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
,
\jmath_{A_{0},1}
pulls back the tautological class
H_{\sigma_{2}}
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
to that of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.

It is clear that
the pullback of the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1|1}
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
is supported on the boundary divisor
\Delta_{1|1}
of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
. By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma

5.6
, we can show that the multiplicity is
1
. Hence,
\jmath_{A_{0},1}
pulls back the boundary class
[\Delta_{1|1}]
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
to that of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.

According to commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

we conclude that
\jmath_{A_{0},1}
pulls back the evaluation class
H_{1}
of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
to that of
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.

Then, by Proposition
5.8
, we conclude that both
\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}
and
\overline{\jmath}_{A_{0},1}^{*}
are isomorphisms. Consequently, the set
\{\Delta_{1|1},H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}}\}
forms a basis for
\operatorname{Pic}\bigl(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)\bigr)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Recall that
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is defined as the pullback of the unbalanced divisor on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
under the forgetful morphism. Fix an
(n-2)
–dimensional isotropic subspace
A_{0}
. Since forgetful morphisms commute with
\jmath_{A_{0},k}
, it suffices to prove that
\overline{\jmath}_{A_{0},0}^{*}(D_{\unb})
equals the unbalanced class
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}},2)
.

Choose a generic
(n+2)
–dimensional vector subspace
V_{n+2}\subset V\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
such that
A_{0}\cap V_{n+2}=\{0\}
. We define
D_{\mathrm{unb}}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
as the closure of the locus of stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]
such that
\dim(H_{\alpha}\cap V_{n+2})\geq 1
. Let
[\beta\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}}]
be a generic point of
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}},2)
. Then
\beta
determines an
(n-1)
–dimensional isotropic subspace
\widehat{A}_{0}
that contains
A_{0}
. Take another
(n-2)
–dimensional isotropic subspace
\check{A}_{0}\subset\widehat{A}_{0}
such that
\dim(\check{A}_{0}\cap V_{n+2})\geq 1
. Then
[\beta\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}}]
is also contained in the image of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{\check{A}_{0}},2)
under
\jmath_{\check{A}_{0},0}
. Notice that the generic point of

\jmath_{\check{A}_{0},0}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{\check{A}_{0}},2))
is contained in the locus of stable maps
[\alpha\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]
such that
\dim(H_{\alpha}\cap V_{n+2})\geq 1
. We conclude that
\jmath_{\check{A}_{0},0}(\beta)=\jmath_{A_{0},0}(\beta)
is contained in
D_{\mathrm{unb}}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
. Since
D_{\unb}\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is irreducible for any
n\geq 2
by Proposition
5.5
, we have that the divisor class

\overline{\jmath}_{A_{0},0}^{*}(D_{\unb})
equals a multiple of
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}},2)
.

Observing that
\jmath_{A_{0},0}^{*}
maps the basis elements
\Delta_{1|1}
and
H_{\sigma_{2}}
of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
to their respective counterparts on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(2,4),2)
, we conclude that
\overline{\jmath}_{A_{0},0}^{*}(D_{\unb})
must equal
D_{\unb}
on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)_{A_{0}},2)
.

The proof of Proposition
5.11
is complete.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

For every
n\geq 2
, the cone of effective divisors on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is given by

\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\ =\ \bigl\langle H_{1},\Delta_{1|1},D_{\unb}\bigr\rangle.

Proof.

Let
E_{n}\subset N^{1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))_{\mathbb{R}}
be the cone generated by
H_{1}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
, and
D_{\unb}
, whose imaages under the isomorphism
\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}
are
H_{1}
,
\Delta_{1|1}
,
H_{\sigma_{2}}
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
, respectively. It is clear that
E_{n}\subset\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
.

By Lemma
5.1

\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is covered by the subvarieties

Y_{A_{0}}\ :=\ \jmath_{A_{0},1}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))\ \subset\ \overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2),

where
A_{0}
are isotropic
(n-2)
–planes.
For any prime divisors
[D]\in\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,k}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
, we may choose
A_{0}
such that
Y_{A_{0}}\not\subset\Supp(D)
.
Then
\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}[D]\ =\ [D\cap Y_{A_{0}}]\in\operatorname{Eff}(Y_{A_{0}})\cong\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))
. By Theorem
5.9

\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))=\bigl\langle H_{1},\Delta_{1|1},D^{\mathrm{unb}}\bigr\rangle
, and by Proposition
5.11
the pullback
\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}
is an isomorphism sending
E_{n}
onto this cone.
Thus
\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}[D]\in\jmath_{A_{0},1}^{*}(E_{n})
implies
[D]\in E_{n}
. Therefore
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\subset E_{n}
.
∎

Fix a general hyperplane section
H\subset\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
. Let
T\subset\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
be the divisor of conics tangent to
H
. We will also denote by
T
its pullback to
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
. Equivalently,
T
is the locus of pointed maps
[f\colon(C,x_{1})\to\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)]
such that the image curve

f(C)
is tangent to the fixed hyperplane section.

Theorem 5.13
.

For every
n\geq 2
the nef cone of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is given by

\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))=\langle H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}},T\rangle.

Proof.

The class
H_{1}=\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)
is nef for
\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)
is ample. By
[
17
, Proposition 6.11]
the nef cone of
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
is generated by
H_{\sigma_{2}}
and
T
. Thus
\langle H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}},T\rangle\subset\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
.

We first assume that
n=2
. Take an arbitrary nef divisor class
[D]\in\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2))
. By (6.12)
in
[
17
]
,
H_{\sigma_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{1|1}+D_{unb}
and
T=\Delta_{1|1}+D_{unb}
, and hence we can write

D\equiv aH_{1}+bH_{\sigma_{2}}+cT.

According to Theorem
5.9
, it is clear that
a\geq 0
.

By
[
17
, Proposition 6.11]
, the divisor
H_{\sigma_{2}}
induces a birational contraction
f_{H_{\sigma_{2}}}\colon\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)\rightarrow Z
whose exceptional locus is the divisor
Q^{2}(1)
parameterizing double covers of a line in
\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
.
Let
\ell\subset\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
be a general line, and let
\Gamma\subset Q^{2}(1)
be a curve contained in the fiber of

f_{H_{\sigma_{2}}}
over the point of
Z
corresponding to
\ell
, so
\Gamma
varies the double cover while keeping
the image line
\ell
fixed. Choose a lift
\widetilde{\Gamma}\subset\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
of
\Gamma
such that the marked point
maps to a fixed point of
\ell
. Then
H_{1}\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}=0
and
H_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}=0
, and hence
c\geq 0
.

By
[
17
, Proposition 6.11]
again, the divisor
T
induces a birational morphism
f_{T}\colon\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)\rightarrow W
which contracts the locus of maps with reducible
domain
[C_{1}\cup C_{2},\alpha]
to
\alpha(C_{1}\cap C_{2})
.
Take a point
p\in f_{T}(\Delta_{1|1})\subset\operatorname{LG}(2,4)
, and let
\Gamma\subset\Delta_{1|1}
be a curve contained in the fiber of

f_{T}
over
p
. For the family of curves
\Gamma
,
take the mark points to be
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
and then stabilize the universal curves. Then we have a lift
\widetilde{\Gamma}\subset\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,4),2)
of
\Gamma
such that the marked point
maps to the fixed point
p
. It is clear that
H_{1}\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}=T\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}=0
, and hence
b\geq 0
.

Finally, to get the claim for any
n\geq 2
it is enough to argue as in the proof of Theorem
5.12
.
∎

H_{1}
\Delta_{1|1}
D_{\unb}
H_{\sigma_{2}}
T

A
2
–dimensional section of
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
(light grey) and
\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
(dark grey).

Proposition 5.14
.

The anticanonical divisor of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
is given by

-K_{\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcll}H_{1}+\frac{5}{2}H_{\sigma_{2}}+\frac{3}{4}\Delta_{1|1}&\equiv&H_{1}+H_{\sigma_{2}}+\frac{3}{2}T&\text{if }n=2\\
H_{1}+\frac{n+2}{2}H_{\sigma_{2}}+\frac{6-n}{4}\Delta_{1|1}&\equiv&H_{1}+(n-2)H_{\sigma_{2}}+\frac{6-n}{2}T&\text{if }n\geq 3\end{array}\right..

Proof.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
be the smooth Kontsevich stack of conics in
\mathrm{LG}(n,2n)
,
\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}
,
\overline{T}
,
\overline{\Delta}_{1|1}
,
\overline{D}_{\mathrm{unb}}
the divisors on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
corresponding to
H_{\sigma_{2}}
,
T
,
\Delta_{1|1}
,
D_{\mathrm{unb}}
respectively.
By
[
17
, Proposition 6.15]
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\overline{\Delta}_{1|1}\equiv 2(\overline{T}-\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}),\quad\overline{D}_{\unb}\equiv\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{T},\quad-K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)}\equiv 5\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}-5\overline{D}_{\unb}&\text{if }n=2;\\
\overline{\Delta}_{1|1}\equiv 2(\overline{T}-\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}),\quad\overline{D}_{\unb}\equiv 2\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}-\overline{T},\quad-K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)}\equiv 5\overline{H}_{\sigma_{2}}+\frac{n-7}{2}\overline{D}_{\unb}&\text{if }n\geq 3.\end{array}

Let
\phi_{2}:\overline{M}_{0,2}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)\to\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
be the morphism forgetting the second marking, and let
s_{1}
be the section corresponding to the first marking. The cotangent line class is
\psi_{1}:=c_{1}(s_{1}^{*}\omega_{\phi_{2}})
, where
\omega_{\phi_{2}}
is the relative dualizing sheaf of
\phi_{2}
.
Let
\pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
be the morphism forgetting the marking. By
[
21
, Theorem 1.1]
, the canonical bundle formula reads

K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)}\ \equiv\ \pi^{*}K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)}+\psi_{1}\qquad\text{in }\operatorname{Pic}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))\otimes\mathbb{Q}.

On a generic fiber
\pi^{-1}([f])\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
(moving only the marking on a fixed degree
2
map
f
), we have
\deg(H_{1})=2
and
\deg(\psi_{1})=\deg(\omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})=-2
, while
T
is pulled back from the unpointed space so it has degree
0
on the fiber. Hence
\psi_{1}\equiv-H_{1}+\pi^{*}(D)
for some divisor
D
downstairs.

To identify
D
, we fix a Plücker hyperplane section
H\subset\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
with defining linear form
\ell
. On the open locus where the domain is irreducible, for a pointed map
(f,x)
we can choose a local coordinate
z
on the source at the marked point
x
(so
z(x)=0
) and write the pullback

(\ell\circ f)(z)=a+bz+cz^{2}.

Intrinsicly,
a
is the evaluation at the marking, hence a local trivialization of
\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)
(i.e. of
H_{1}
);
b
is the first jet at the marking, hence lies in
\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)\otimes\psi_{1}
;
c
is the second jet, hence lies in
\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)}(1)\otimes\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}
. Equivalently, under the reparametrization
z\mapsto\lambda z
(automorphisms fixing the marked point) we have
b\mapsto\lambda b
,
c\mapsto\lambda^{2}c
, and under rescaling
\ell\mapsto\kappa\ell
we have
(a,b,c)\mapsto\kappa(a,b,c)
.
Therefore the discriminant

\operatorname{Disc}(\ell\circ f):=b^{2}-4ac

is a well-defined global section of

(\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{LG}(1))^{\otimes 2}\otimes\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}\cong\mathcal{O}(2H_{1}+2\psi_{1}).

By definition of the tangency divisor
T
(the locus where
\ell\circ f
does not have two distinct smooth zeros on the domain), we have

T=\operatorname{div}(\operatorname{Disc}(\ell\circ f)),

hence
\mathcal{O}(T)\cong\mathcal{O}(2H_{1}+2\psi_{1})
and so
\psi_{1}\equiv-H_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}T
.

Note that the locus of points of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
with non trivial stabilizer has codimension
>1
. These numerical equivalences descend unchanged to the coarse moduli space
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
, and rewriting them in the basis
\{H_{1},H_{\sigma_{2}},\Delta_{1|1}\}
gives the stated formulas.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

The Kontsevich space
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
is Fano if and only if
2\leq n\leq 5
, and weak Fano for
n=6
. Furthermore,
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
is a Mori dream space for every
n\geq 2
.

Proof.

From the explicit determination of
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
and
\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
in Theorems
5.12
,
5.13
it follows that
\operatorname{Mov}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))=\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
. Indeed, if
D
is effective but not nef, then
D
has negative intersection with one of the curve classes used
to cut out the nef cone in Theorem
5.13
, which move in a divisor (either

\Delta_{1|1}
or
D_{\mathrm{unb}}
);
hence
D
is not movable.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2))
admits a Mori chamber decomposition with exactly three chambers. Therefore
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
is a Mori dream space for every
n\geq 2
. The rest of the claim follows from Proposition
5.14
.
∎

By Corollary
5.15
, we have:

Corollary 5.16
.

For
2\leq n\leq 5
, the Kontsevich space
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\mathrm{LG}(n,2n),2)
is a Fano variety of index
1
.

Proof.

Let
X=\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
with
2\leq n\leq 5
.
It suffices to prove that
-K_{X}
is not divisible by any integer
m\geq 2
in
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
.

Consider the test subvarieties
\iota_{p,q}:\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3})\hookrightarrow X
in Lemma
5.6
, where
p,q
are two general points.
Then,

\iota_{p,q}^{*}(H_{1})\equiv H,\qquad\iota_{p,q}^{*}(H_{\sigma_{2}})\equiv H-E_{p}-E_{q},\qquad\iota_{p,q}^{*}(T)\equiv 2(H-E_{p}-E_{q}).

By Proposition
5.14
, we have that

\iota_{p,q}^{*}(-K_{X})=5H-4E_{p}-4E_{q}.
The class
5H-4E_{p}-4E_{q}\in\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Bl}_{\{p,q\}}(Q^{3}))
is primitive since
\gcd(5,4,4)=1
. The proof is complete.
∎

The following is the generalization of
[
17
, Corollary 6.18]
(the case
n=2
) to every
n\geq 2
.

Proposition 5.17
.

Automorphisms of
\overline{M}_{0,0}\big(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2\big)
are induced by that of the target
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
:

\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\cong\PSp_{2n},\,\,\forall\,n\geq 2.

Proof.

We may assume
n\geq 3
. By
[
17
, Theorem 6.14]
, the effective cone
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
is generated by the two extremal rays

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle\Delta^{n}\rangle
and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle D^{n}_{\mathrm{unb}}\rangle
,
and the nef cone
\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
is generated by

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle H^{n}_{\sigma_{2}}\rangle
and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle T^{n}\rangle
. For any
\varphi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
,
\varphi^{*}
preserves both
\operatorname{Eff}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
and
\operatorname{Nef}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
, hence it must fix each extremal ray.

Consider the birational model
X_{n}
associated in
[
17
, Theorem 6.14]
to the Mori chamber delimited by

H^{n}_{\sigma_{2}}
and
D^{n}_{\mathrm{unb}}
; by functoriality of the chamber decomposition under
\varphi^{*}
,
the automorphism
\varphi
induces an automorphism of
X_{n}
over the symplectic Grassmannian parametrizing
(n-2)
–dimensional isotropic subspaces of a
2n
–dimensional symplectic vector space
\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
. Moreover,
[
17
, Theorem 6.14]
identifies
X_{n}
as a relative Hilbert scheme
X_{n}:=\operatorname{Hilb}_{2t+1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,\mathcal{Q}_{n}))\rightarrow\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is the rank
4
universal quotient bundle on
\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
. Since
\varphi^{*}[D^{n}_{\mathrm{unb}}]=[D^{n}_{\mathrm{unb}}]
,
\varphi
descends to an automorphism

\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n))
. This defines a group homomorphism

\rho\colon\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)).

Take an arbitrary automorphism
\phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
such that
\rho(\phi)={\rm Id}
.
Since the fibers of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{2t+1}(\operatorname{LG}(2,\mathcal{Q}_{n}))\rightarrow\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n)
are isomorphic to
G(3,5)
, the induced automorphism of
X_{n}
yields an automorphism of the underlying rank
4
bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
. By
[
58
]

\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is a simple homogeneous vector bundle, hence
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}_{n})=\mathbb{C}^{*}
. We can thus conclude that
\rho
is injective.

Finally, since the Dynkin diagram of type
C_{n}
has no nontrivial automorphisms,
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{SG}(n-2,2n))=\PSp_{2n}
by
[
23
]
. On the other hand,
\PSp_{2n}=\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))
acts naturally on
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
. Hence
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\cong\PSp_{2n}
.
∎

The following is the pointed analogue of Proposition
5.17
.

Proposition 5.18
.

Automorphisms of
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
are induced by that of
\operatorname{LG}(n,2n)
:

\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\cong\PSp_{2n},\,\,\forall\,n\geq 2.

Proof.

By Theorems
5.12
,
5.13
, any

\varphi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
preserves the extremal rays of these cones. In particular,
\varphi^{*}
preserves the ray

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle H_{1}\rangle
, hence the complete linear system
|mH_{1}|
for
m\gg 0
.
Because
H_{1}=\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}H
, the morphism defined by
|mH_{1}|
is exactly
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
, so there is a unique

\widehat{\varphi}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n))
such that
\operatorname{ev}_{1}\circ\varphi=\widehat{\varphi}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{1}
.
Moreover,
H_{\sigma_{2}}
and
T
on
\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
are pullbacks via the forgetful morphism

\phi_{1}
from
\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
, so preserving the corresponding contraction yields a unique

\bar{\varphi}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
with
\phi_{1}\circ\varphi=\bar{\varphi}\circ\phi_{1}
. Therefore,
\varphi
induces an automorphism
\bar{\varphi}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,0}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
.

Identifying
\bar{\varphi}
with an element of
\PSp_{2n}
via Proposition
5.17
, we derive a natural homomorphism

\chi\colon\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))\rightarrow\PSp_{2n}\qquad\chi(\varphi):=\bar{\varphi}.

Moreover,

\varphi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2))
and
\bar{\varphi}=\chi(\varphi)
fit into the following commutative diagrams.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Here
\phi_{1}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
are the forgetful and evaluation morphisms, respectively.

Now let
\varphi\in\ker(\chi)
. Then for every point
[f,x]\in\overline{M}_{0,1}(\operatorname{LG}(n,2n),2)
, we have

\phi_{1}(\varphi([f,x]))=\phi_{1}([f,x])=[f],\qquad\operatorname{ev}_{1}(\varphi([f,x]))=\operatorname{ev}_{1}([f,x])=f(x).

That is,
\varphi([f,x])
belongs to the intersection

\pi^{-1}([f])\cap\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{-1}(f(x))
, which consists of the unique point
[f,x]
. Consequently
\varphi([f,x])=[f,x]
for every
[f,x]
, and thus
\chi
is an isomorphism.
∎

6.
Kausz–Type Compactifications related to Orthogonal Grassmannians

Based on
[
26
,
25
]
, one can extend the mechanism to orthogonal Grassmannians. Let
W
be an
n
–dimensional complex vector space and set
V:=W\oplus W^{\vee}
with its canonical quadratic form defined by
Q(w,\alpha):=\alpha(w)
.
We denote by
\operatorname{OG}(V,Q)
the orthogonal Grassmannian parametrising maximal isotropic subspaces, and by
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(V,Q)
the connected component of
\operatorname{OG}(V,Q)
that contains the maximal isotropic subspace
W\oplus\{0\}\subset W\oplus W^{\vee}
.

The first canonical embedding of
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(V,Q)
can be given as follows (see
[
39
]
). Let
C(W\oplus W^{\vee},Q)
be the Clifford algebra with a linear map

i\colon W\oplus W^{\vee}\rightarrow C(W\oplus W^{\vee},Q)
such that
[i(x)]^{2}=Q(x)
. The spinor representation
\rho\colon C(W\oplus W^{\vee},Q)\rightarrow{\rm End}(\bigwedge W^{\vee})
defined by
\rho(w,\alpha)=l_{\alpha}+\iota_{w}
splits into half-spinor representations
\bigwedge^{\rm even}W^{\vee}
,
\bigwedge^{\rm odd}W^{\vee}
, where

l_{\alpha}
is the left multiplication
and
i_{w}
is the derivation.
A spinor
\phi\in\bigwedge^{\rm even}W^{\vee}
is pure if its annihilator
L_{\phi}:=\{x\in W\oplus W^{\vee}:x\cdot\phi=0\}
is a maximal isotropic subspace. Each
L\in\operatorname{OG}^{+}(V,Q)
corresponds to a unique pure spinor line

[\phi_{L}]
. we have the spinor embedding

(6.1)

e_{\operatorname{OG}^{+}}:\operatorname{OG}^{+}(V,Q)\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{\rm even}W^{\vee}\big).

Similarly, we can define a
\mathscr{G}:=\operatorname{GL}(W^{\vee})\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
–equivariant rational map

(6.2)

\mathrm{KO}_{n}\colon\operatorname{OG}^{+}(V,Q)\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}\big(\bigoplus\limits_{k=0}^{[n/2]}\bigwedge\limits^{2k}W^{\vee}\big)\times\prod\limits_{k=0}^{[n/2]}\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\limits^{2k}W^{\vee}\big).

The Kausz–type compactification
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
of the open
\mathscr{G}
–orbit in
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)
is defined as the closure of the graph of
\mathrm{KO}_{n}
.
By projection to the first factor, we define

\mathrm{R}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TO}_{n}\longrightarrow\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)\subset\mathbb{P}\big(\bigoplus\limits_{k=0}^{[n/2]}\bigwedge\limits^{2k}W^{\vee}\big).

The
space of complete skew–forms
is the closure of the graph

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}W^{\vee}\big)&\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}W^{\vee}\big)\times\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}W^{\vee}\big)\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}\bigwedge\nolimits^{[n/2]}W^{\vee}\big).\end{split}

We denote this smooth projective variety by
\mathsf{CS}_{n}
. Note that the first boundary divisor
D_{0}^{-}\subset\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{CS}_{n}
, and so is
D_{0}^{+}
. Then by projection onto
D_{0}^{\pm}
, we can define a
\mathscr{G}
–equivariant flat morphism

(6.3)

\mathrm{P}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TO}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathsf{CS}_{n}.

As in Proposition
2.10
,

\mathrm{R}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TO}_{n}\to\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)
can be written as an iterated blow–up as follows.
Under a natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
–action similar to (
2.1
), the connected components of the fixed point scheme of
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)
are

\begin{split}&\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{matrix}0&X\\
Y&0\\
\end{matrix}\right)\in\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)\right|\footnotesize\begin{matrix}X\,\,{\rm is\,\,a\,\,}2k\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank}\,\,2k\,\\
Y\,\,{\rm is\,\,an\,\,}(n-2k)\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank}\,\,(n-2k)\\
\end{matrix}\right\}\,,\,\,\,\,0\leq k\leq[n/2],\\
\end{split}

and the stable, unstable submanifolds are respectively

(6.4)

\begin{split}&\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}^{+}:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{matrix}0&X\\
Y&W\\
\end{matrix}\right)\in\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)\right|\footnotesize\begin{matrix}X\,\,{\rm is\,\,a\,\,}2k\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank\,\,}2k\,\\
Y\,\,{\rm is\,\,an\,\,}(n-2k)\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank\,\,}(n-2k)\,\\
\end{matrix}\right\},\\
&\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}^{-}:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{matrix}Z&X\\
Y&0\\
\end{matrix}\right)\in\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)\right|{\footnotesize\begin{matrix}X\,\,{\rm is\,\,a\,\,}2k\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank\,\,}2k\,\\
Y\,\,{\rm is\,\,an\,\,}(n-2k)\times n\,\,{\rm matrix\,\,of\,\,rank\,\,}(n-2k)\,\\
\end{matrix}}\right\}\,.\end{split}

Define
Z^{+}_{n-2k}=\overline{\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}^{+}}
and
Z^{-}_{2k}=\overline{\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}^{-}}
.
When
n
is even,
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
are obtained from
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)
by sequentially blowing up the strict transforms of
Z_{0}^{-},Z_{2}^{-},\dots,Z_{n-2}^{-},Z_{0}^{+},Z_{2}^{+},\dots,Z_{n-2}^{+}
(in that order); when
n
is odd,
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
are obtained by sequentially blowing up the strict transforms of
Z_{0}^{-},Z_{2}^{-},\dots,Z_{n-3}^{-},Z_{1}^{+},Z_{3}^{+},\dots,Z_{n-2}^{+}
.

The boundary divisors of
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
are

\cup_{0\leq k\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1}D_{k}^{+}\cup_{0\leq k\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1}D_{k}^{-}
, where

D_{k}^{-}:=\overline{(\mathrm{R}_{n})^{-1}({\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2k,2k)}^{-}})}\,\,{\rm and}\,\,D_{k}^{+}:=\overline{(\mathrm{R}_{n})^{-1}({\underline{\mathcal{V}}_{(n-2[\frac{n}{2}]+2k,2[\frac{n}{2}]-2k)}^{+}})}.

We introduce the Mille Crêpes coordinates
for
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
as follows. Choose a basis
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
for
W
and let
\{e_{1}^{*},\cdots,e_{n}^{*}\}
denote its dual basis in
W^{\vee}
. For any even integers
\ell
with
0\leq\ell\leq n
, we have the following affine coordinate chart around the maximal isotropic subspace
L_{12\cdots\ell}
generated by
e_{\ell+1},\cdots,e_{n},e^{*}_{1},\cdots,e^{*}_{\ell}
.

(6.5)

U^{\prime}_{12\cdots l}:=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}Z\\
-X^{\!\top}\\
\end{matrix}\right.\hskip-7.94974pt\begin{matrix}&\hfill\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

&\hfill\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

&\hfill\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{4.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 9.69 L 4.15 -2.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{4.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 9.69 L 4.15 -2.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

where
Y
,
Z
are anti–symmetric. Such coordinate charts cover
\operatorname{OG}^{+}(n,2n)
up to permutation.
We define

\underline{\mathbb{I}}_{n}^{k}:=\big\{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{2k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2k}:1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{2k}\leq n\}\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,0\leq k\leq[n/2].

Denote by
\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
the ideal sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{OG}^{+}(n,2n)}
generated by
\{\left(e_{\mathrm{OG}^{+}}\right)^{*}z_{I}:{I\in\underline{\mathbb{I}}_{n}^{k}}\}
. Similarly,

Lemma 6.6
.

For any integers
k,l
such that
0\leq k\leq[n/2]
,
0\leq l\leq n
, the restriction of
\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
to the affine coordinate chart
U^{\prime}_{12\cdots l}
is generated by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\operatorname{Pf}(Z_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l-2k}})\}_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l-2k}\leq l},&2k<l\\
\{\operatorname{Pf}(W_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2k-l}})\}_{l+1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{2k-l}\leq n},&2k>l\\
1,&2k=l\\
\end{array}\right..

Here
\operatorname{Pf}
is the Pfaffian of a anti–symmetric matrix.

Then we have the open covering

\mathcal{TO}_{n}\ =\bigcup_{g\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{TO}_{n})}\;\ \bigcup_{{\rm even}\,\,\ell=0}^{[n/2]}g(A_{\ell}),

where

A_{\ell}\ \cong\ \mathbb{A}^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}

with regular coordinates

\bigl\{a_{\ell,i}^{+}\bigr\}_{[\frac{n}{2}]-\frac{\ell}{2}\leq i\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1},\quad\bigl\{a_{\ell,i}^{-}\bigr\}_{\frac{\ell}{2}\leq i\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1},\quad\bigl\{b_{\ell,1},\dots,b_{\ell,\frac{n(n-1)}{2}-[\frac{n}{2}]}\bigr\},

satisfying the following properties:

-

On each
A_{\ell}
, for
[\frac{n}{2}]-\frac{\ell}{2}\leq i\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1
(resp.
\frac{\ell}{2}\leq i\leq[\frac{n}{2}]-1
), the intersection

D_{i}^{+}\cap A_{\ell}
(resp.
D_{i}^{-}\cap A_{\ell}
) is given by the vanishing
of a single coordinate
a_{\ell,i}^{+}
(resp.
a_{\ell,i}^{-}
); otherwise, the intersection is empty.

-

The coordinates
b_{\ell,1},\dots,b_{\ell,\frac{n(n-1)}{2}-[\frac{n}{2}]}
are
horizontal coordinates along the fibers of (
6.3
).

Consequently, we obtain the following results.

Theorem 6.7
.

The variety
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is smooth and projective. The group
\mathscr{G}

acts on
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
with a dense open orbit isomorphic to a homogeneous space
\mathscr{G}/\mathscr{H}
, where
\mathscr{H}\subset\mathscr{G}
is the stabilizer of a fixed skew–form on
W^{\vee}
of maximal rank. The complement of this open orbit is a divisor with
2[\frac{n}{2}]
smooth irreducible components

D_{0}^{-},\dots,D_{[\frac{n}{2}]-1}^{-},\ D_{0}^{+},\dots,D_{[\frac{n}{2}]-1}^{+}.

In particular,
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is a smooth projective spherical
\mathscr{G}
–variety.

Theorem 6.8
.

The variety
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is weak Fano for every
n\geq 2
. Moreover,
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is Fano if
and only if
2\leq n\leq 5
.

Theorem 6.9
.

For every
n\geq 2
we have

H^{i}\bigl(\mathcal{TO}_{n},T_{\mathcal{TO}_{n}}\bigr)=0\qquad\text{for all }i>0.

In particular,
\mathcal{TO}_{n}
is locally rigid, namely, it has no local deformations.

Theorem 6.10
.

The universal family of the Hilbert quotient
\mathbf{OG}^{+}(V\oplus V^{*})\!/\!/\mathbb{G}_{m}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{P}_{n}\colon\mathcal{TO}_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{CS}_{n}
. In particular, all three Hilbert quotients in
[
60
]
have smooth universal families.

We refer to
[
25
]
for a more general treatment. Note that our notation for boundary divisors is shifted relative to
[
25
]
; specifically, our
D^{\pm}_{i}
corresponds to
D^{\pm}_{i+1}
therein.
